[[Block:Dialogue Evaluation]]

MC1. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 41-year-old female presented with right-sided tonsillar pain radiating to the ipsilateral neck. On oral examination, a tender and palpable right styloid process is noticed in the right tonsillar fossa. Her computerized tomography neck scan neck demonstrated an enlarged and calcified right styloid process of 44 mm (Figure 1). She underwent right styloidectomy via tonsillectomy approach by cold steel dissection method. Intraoperatively, deep dissection was done to expose the styloid process and there was some difficulty in cutting the styloid process since it was calcified otherwise operation was uneventful. She had bleeding from the oral cavity on the third POD which was small and stopped spontaneously. On 7th POD, she had short-lasting but abrupt and profuse secondary haemorrhage leading to haemorrhagic shock and managed with transfused with 3 units of fresh whole blood. Re-exploration under general anaesthesia, except granulation tissue and minor ooze from breached constrictor, no other major bleeding was seen. She was discharged on the tenth POD. She again came with profuse and short-lasting bleeding from the right side of the oral cavity on 13th POD which was stopped on arrival. She was readmitted and a contrast-enhanced computerized tomography scan (CECT) of the neck was done due to the redcurrant and unusual nature of the bleeding. CECT neck showed contrast-filled dilatation of a branch of the right facial artery in the right palatine tonsillar fossa within the operative bedlikely pseudoaneurysm. She was referred to the centre where endovascular service was available. Pseudoaneurysm which was clearly seen at the bed of the right tonsillar fossa during angiography (Figure 2) was successfully coiled and embolized (Figure 3). After that, the patient did not bleed and was discharged after 2 days. On a 6-month follow-up, the patient was normal.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, please have a seat. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Good morning, Doctor. For a while now, I&#x27;ve had this pain on the right side of my throat. It&#x27;s mostly in my right tonsil area, but it also radiates down my neck on the same side. **Doctor:** Okay. Let me take a look. (Doctor examines the patient&#x27;s throat) I see. It&#x27;s tender when I press here, in your right tonsillar fossa, and I can feel a palpable styloid process. I think we need to get a CT scan of your neck to get a better picture. **(After CT Scan)** **Doctor:** Okay, I&#x27;ve reviewed your CT scan. It shows that your right styloid process is enlarged and calcified, measuring about 44 millimeters. It&#x27;s likely causing the pain you&#x27;ve been experiencing. We call this Eagle syndrome. I recommend a styloidectomy. It involves removing the elongated styloid process. **Patient:** How would that be done? **Doctor:** We would do it through a tonsillectomy approach, removing the styloid process through the area where your tonsil used to be. It will involve careful dissection. **(After initial surgery)** **Doctor:** The surgery went well, although the styloid process was quite calcified, which made it a little challenging to cut. Otherwise, it was uneventful. **Patient:** That&#x27;s good to hear. **(Three days later)** **Patient:** Doctor, I started bleeding from my mouth. **Doctor:** How much bleeding was there? **Patient:** It was a little bit, but it stopped on its own. **(Four days later)** **Patient:** Doctor, Im bleeding again, and this time it is a lot worse. It was a sudden gush, and I felt really faint. **Doctor:** Okay, we need to get you admitted right away. We&#x27;ll run some tests and give you a blood transfusion. We will need to re-explore the surgical site under anesthesia. **(After re-exploration)** **Doctor:** We re-explored the surgical site. We found some granulation tissue and a minor ooze, but nothing to explain the amount of bleeding you had. We&#x27;ll keep you under observation for a few more days. **(Three days later)** **Patient:** Doctor, I&#x27;m bleeding again! It&#x27;s just like before, a sudden, heavy gush of blood. **Doctor:** We need to figure out what&#x27;s causing this. Given the nature of the bleeding, I&#x27;m concerned about a possible pseudoaneurysm. We&#x27;ll do a contrast-enhanced CT scan of your neck. **(After CECT Scan)** **Doctor:** The CT scan shows a contrast-filled dilation of a branch of the right facial artery in the right palatine tonsillar fossa - a pseudoaneurysm. This is likely the cause of the bleeding. We need to refer you to a center with endovascular services so they can embolize the pseudoaneurysm. **(After Embolization)** **Doctor:** The angiogram clearly showed the pseudoaneurysm. The interventional radiologist successfully coiled and embolized it. You should be able to go home in a couple of days. **Patient:** That&#x27;s a relief! **(Six months later - Follow up)** **Doctor:** How have you been feeling since the embolization? **Patient:** I&#x27;m feeling great, Doctor! No more bleeding, and the pain is completely gone. Thank you so much!

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC2. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 33-year-old Turkish woman presented with a decrease in psychomotor activation (PMA) and self-care, dysphoria, insomnia, and persecutory delusions. She was hospitalized with the preliminary diagnosis of &quot;major depression with psychotic features,&quot; according to DSM-IV criteria. The patient was not addicted to alcohol or any illicit substances. Her score on the 17-item Hamilton Depression Rating Scale (HAMD-17) was 46, indicating very severe depression. Full laboratory investigations were in the normal range. She was commenced on haloperidol, 20 mg/day, and biperiden, 4 mg/day, and was given seven sessions of ECT, as described in detail below. Increases in PMA, euphoria and grandiosity were observed after the seventh ECT treatment. Subsequently ECT was stopped, and lithium, 900 mg/day, was added to the treatment. Her score on the Young Mania Rating Scale (YMRS) was 28, and her score on the HAMD-17 was less than 7. Her mania and depression severity were, respectively, moderate and normal. The patient was discharged with a prescribed treatment of lithium, 900 mg/day, (serum level: 0.6 mg/dL) and chlorpromazine, 300 mg/day.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, please come in and have a seat. So, tell me what&#x27;s been going on. **Patient:** Well, I&#x27;ve been feeling really down lately. It&#x27;s hard to even get out of bed. I just don&#x27;t have the energy or motivation to do anything, even take care of myself. I&#x27;ve been feeling really sad and hopeless. And I haven&#x27;t been sleeping well at all. **Doctor:** I understand. And have you been experiencing any unusual thoughts or beliefs? **Patient:** Yes, actually. I&#x27;ve been having these strange thoughts that people are out to get me, persecutory delusions. **Doctor:** Okay. Based on what you&#x27;ve told me, and after examining you, I think you may be suffering from major depression with psychotic features. It fits the criteria we use to diagnose it. I want to assure you that you are not alone and we can help you feel better. And just to be clear, you don&#x27;t drink alcohol excessively or use any drugs, right? **Patient:** No, I don&#x27;t. **Doctor:** Alright. So, when you first came in, we did a depression assessment, the Hamilton Depression Rating Scale, and your score was 46. That indicates a very severe level of depression. Your blood work and other lab tests came back normal. We started you on haloperidol and biperiden. And we also started you on a course of ECT, electroconvulsive therapy. We gave you seven sessions. **Patient:** Yes, I remember. **Doctor:** After the seventh session, we noticed a significant change. You started showing increased energy, almost the opposite of before, and you seemed very happy and even a bit grandiose. **Patient:** I did feel much better, but maybe a little too good? **Doctor:** That&#x27;s right. Because of that, we stopped the ECT and added lithium to your medication. Your depression score improved to less than 7. But the mania score was 28, which is moderate. So, we are going to discharge you today. You&#x27;ll be going home with a prescription for lithium, 900 mg per day, and chlorpromazine, 300 mg per day. Your lithium blood level is 0.6 mg/dL. It&#x27;s important to take these medications as prescribed and come back for regular check-ups so we can monitor your progress. How does that sound?

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC3. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 59-year-old woman was admitted to our hospital for treatment in July 2019 due to recurrent cough and sputum accompanied by fever for 6 months and symptom aggravation for 3 months. There was a small amount of white mucus sputum, but no blood in the sputum. The patients body temperature was intermittently elevated up to 40C, and this was occasionally accompanied by night sweats that were independent of chills and diurnal changes. During the period of onset, the patient lost 5 kg in body weight. The patient had no history of hypertension, diabetes, cardiovascular and cerebrovascular diseases, hepatitis, tuberculosis, malaria, surgery, trauma, abdominal discomfort, dysuria, joint pain, or other diseases. She had gone through menopause at age 50 years. An abdominal examination was performed, but no mass was touched. A chest CT showed thickening of the bronchial wall in the left upper lobe and dilatation of the left upper lobe, and it also showed mass flaky shadows in the right liver lobe (Figure 1a). After admission, the laboratory assessment showed a hemoglobin (Hb) level of 104 g/L (normal range: 115150 g/L), a platelet count (PLT) of 376,000/mm3 (normal range: 125,000350,000/mm3), a white blood cell (WBC) count of 15.71  109/L (normal range: 410  109/L), an erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) of 90 mm/hour (normal range: 026 mm/hour), and a C-reactive protein (CRP) level of 40.05 mg/L (normal range: 05 mg/L). Biochemical assays revealed the following: an alkaline phosphatase (ALP) level of 187.4 U/L (normal range: 50135 U/L), a gamma-glutamyltransferase (-GT) level of 209.3 U/L (normal range: 745 U/L), and a serum albumin (ALB) level of 39.1 g/L (normal range: 4055 g/L). Levels of alanine aminotransaminase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), and total bilirubin (TBIL) were within the normal range.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs (pauses to check chart) Jones. Please, have a seat. I understand you&#x27;ve been having some trouble. **Mrs. Jones:** Yes, Doctor. I&#x27;ve been feeling quite unwell for about six months now. It got much worse in the last three. **Doctor:** Can you tell me more about what you&#x27;ve been experiencing? **Mrs. Jones:** Well, I have a recurrent cough with sputum. It&#x27;s mostly white mucus, nothing bloody. And I&#x27;ve been getting fevers, sometimes up to 40 degrees. **Doctor:** Are you experiencing anything else with the fevers? **Mrs. Jones:** Yes, I get terrible night sweats, but not really with chills during the day. Also, I&#x27;ve lost about 5 kilograms recently. **Doctor:** Okay. And before this started, were you generally healthy? Do you have any history of hypertension, diabetes, or heart problems? **Mrs. Jones:** No, nothing like that. No hepatitis, tuberculosis, or anything. I haven&#x27;t had any surgeries or major injuries either. I went through menopause at 50, but that&#x27;s about it. **Doctor:** Alright. Now, I did a physical exam, including your abdomen. I didn&#x27;t feel any unusual masses. We also did a chest CT scan. The scan showed some thickening of the bronchial wall and dilatation in the left upper lobe of your lung. It also showed some mass-like shadows in the right lobe of your liver. **Mrs. Jones:** Oh dear, that doesn&#x27;t sound good. **Doctor:** We also ran some blood tests. Your hemoglobin is a bit low at 104, and your white blood cell count is elevated at 15.71. Your platelets are slightly elevated at 376,000. Your ESR is quite high at 90, and your CRP is also elevated at 40.05, which indicates inflammation. **Mrs. Jones:** What does that all mean? **Doctor:** It means that there is definitely an active inflammatory process in your body. We also checked your liver function. Your alkaline phosphatase is elevated at 187.4, and your gamma-glutamyltransferase is also high at 209.3. Your albumin is a little low at 39.1. Your other liver enzymes, like ALT and AST, and bilirubin were normal. So, with the information we have, we need to do more investigation to find out the cause of your symptoms.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.

MC4. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 75-year-old woman was diagnosed with subarachnoid hemorrhage and a neurosurgeon performed aneurysmal clipping through a unicoronal incision. Two months later, the patient underwent cranioplasty using the cryopreserved autogenous cranial bone. At the sites of the surgical incision, there was a yellowish pus-like discharge and S. epidermidis was identified. Three weeks after cranioplasty, the wound was closed following incision and drainage. Due to tension at the suture sites, however, ischemic necrosis of the flap margin occurred. Conservative management did not improve wound healing. On physical examination, the patient had full-thickness skin defects 12 mm10 mm in size on the scalp, accompanied by focal osteomyelitis (Fig. 1). Twelve weeks after cranioplasty, under local anesthesia, we removed all the dirty granulation tissues located in the dead space under the grafted cranial bone, after bone shaving. One week after debridement, under general anesthesia, we reconstructed the chronic ulcer using a bipedicled pericranial flap where the posterior auricular artery and the left superficial temporal artery served as a pedicle. The patient had a satisfactory treatment outcome, without any notable problems during an 11-month postoperative follow-up period.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. [Patient&#x27;s Last Name]. How are you feeling today? **Patient:** Well, Doctor, things have been a bit up and down since the subarachnoid hemorrhage. **Doctor:** I understand. Let&#x27;s recap everything. You came in at 75 years old, and we diagnosed you with a subarachnoid hemorrhage. Dr. [Neurosurgeon&#x27;s Name] performed the aneurysmal clipping, using an incision across the top of your head. **Patient:** That&#x27;s right. Then, a couple of months later, you did the cranioplasty with the bone they&#x27;d saved. **Doctor:** Yes, we used your own cryopreserved cranial bone. But then, you developed a yellowish discharge from the incision, almost like pus, right? **Patient:** Exactly. It was worrying. **Doctor:** We identified *S. epidermidis* in the discharge. We performed an incision and drainage about three weeks after the cranioplasty, and while it closed the wound, there was too much tension. That unfortunately led to some tissue at the edge of the flap dying off  ischemic necrosis. **Patient:** That&#x27;s when it got really uncomfortable. It just wouldn&#x27;t heal. **Doctor:** That&#x27;s correct. During the physical exam, we saw you had full-thickness skin defects on your scalp, about 12 by 10 millimeters in size, and we also identified a small area of bone infection, focal osteomyelitis. **Patient:** Yes, I remember. **Doctor:** So, about twelve weeks after the cranioplasty, we did a procedure to clean everything up. Under local anesthesia, we removed all the infected tissue and shaved down some of the bone. **Patient:** That was a relief. **Doctor:** Then, a week later, under general anesthesia, we used a bipedicled pericranial flap  basically, skin and tissue from your scalp  to cover the ulcer. We used the posterior auricular artery and the left superficial temporal artery to keep it supplied with blood. **Patient:** And thankfully, that seemed to do the trick. **Doctor:** Yes, and I am happy to report that your outcome has been very good. We&#x27;ve been monitoring you for the last eleven months, and everything looks stable and healthy. We haven&#x27;t seen any significant problems. **Patient:** Oh, thank you, Doctor.

3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC5. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 25-year-old man was diagnosed with subdural hemorrhage and underwent decompression via a pterional approach by a neurosurgeon. When his symptoms improved, the patient underwent cranioplasty using the cryopreserved autogenous cranial bone. The ulcer occurred along the incision line. Despite being treated with ambulatory treatments, it had still not healed at postoperative four months. At the time of the outpatient visit, the patient presented with full-thickness scalp defects 20 mm10 mm in size, where the discolored bone was exposed. In addition, the hair and follicles were also exposed in the subcutaneous layer adjacent to the ulcer (Fig. 3). After identifying methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus, the patient received nine days of pre-treatment. Following the resection of the ulcer and the adjacent scar tissue, there was a discoloration in the overall layer of the grafted calvarial bone. We removed this layer, including its inner table. By using the left occipital artery, the right supraocular artery, and the supratrochlear artery as a pedicle, we performed a bipedicled pericranial flap. In addition, we elevated a unipedicled pericranial flap, where the superficial temporal artery served as a pedicle, to cover the defects after the removal of the cranial bone. Until postoperative four months, the patient had good aesthetic outcomes without developing any notable complications.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, good morning. So, you&#x27;re here for a follow-up regarding the surgery you had after your subdural hemorrhage, right? You&#x27;re 25, if I remember correctly. **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. The neurosurgeon did a great job with the decompression through the pterional approach and the cranioplasty with my own bone that was cryopreserved. I felt much better after those. **Doctor:** I&#x27;m glad to hear that. However, I understand you&#x27;ve been having some trouble with the incision site? **Patient:** Yes, I developed an ulcer along the incision. I&#x27;ve been trying different creams and dressings, ambulatory treatments, but it&#x27;s been four months now, and it&#x27;s still not healing. **Doctor:** Okay. Let&#x27;s take a look. (Examines the area) I see. It looks like you have a full-thickness scalp defect, about 20 by 10 millimeters. The bone underneath is discolored and is exposed. I can also see the hair follicles exposed around the edges of the ulcer. We took a sample earlier, and it came back positive for methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus, MRSA. So, we pre-treated it for nine days. Now, to fix this, what we did was remove the ulcer and the surrounding scar tissue. **Patient:** Okay. **Doctor:** We noticed that the grafted bone underneath was also discolored, so we removed that discolored layer, including its inner table. Then, we used a technique called a bipedicled pericranial flap, using the left occipital artery, the right supraocular artery, and the supratrochlear artery as a blood supply. We also used another flap, a unipedicled pericranial flap supplied by the superficial temporal artery, to cover the remaining defect after removing the affected bone. **Patient:** And how did it go? **Doctor:** Everything went well. It&#x27;s been four months since that procedure, and you&#x27;ve had a good aesthetic outcome, without any complications. How are you feeling now? **Patient:** That&#x27;s great to hear! It looks good and feels much better. Thank you, Doctor.

1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.

MC6. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 63-year-old female patient was admitted to our hospital because of rapidly progressive leg accentuated atrophic tetraparesis and paresthesia almost one year after alloHSCT for high risk myelodysplastic syndrome (for details see Table 1). Post-transplant complications had included transient neuropathic complaints by day 12 which recovered spontaneously, severe acute GvHD of the skin by day 31 and moderate GvHD of the gut by day 49, both transient. An atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome was diagnosed 3 months after alloHSCT and successfully treated with plasmapheresis and eculizumab. A simultaneously observed tetraparesis with electrophysiological identification of an axonal polyneuropathy was interpreted as critical illness polyneuropathy. Almost 1 year after alloHSCT progressive leg weakness with loss of walking ability and increasing numbness of all limbs over two months led to a detailed neurological examination. Nerve conduction studies revealed a severe axonal polyneuropathy with a significant deterioration compared to the previous measurement. Because of subjective and objective xerophthalmia and positive SSA(Ro)-autoantibodies a minor salivary gland biopsy was performed and uncovered a sialadenitis with more than one focus per 4 mm2 (Fig. 1D), but no fibrotic areas as seen in chronic GvHD-related dry eye disease15. Hence, chronic inflammatory polyneuropathy associated with Sjgrens syndrome was diagnosed12,16. Since then, with the exception of a single interruption due to a severe infection, she received monthly intravenous immunoglobulin treatment, whereby clinical symptoms and electrophysiological measurements improved. The polyneuropathy was stable at last follow-up, 29 months after initiation of immunoglobulin treatment.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. [Patient&#x27;s Last Name]. Please, have a seat. I see from your chart that you&#x27;re here for a follow-up on your polyneuropathy. **Patient:** Yes, Doctor. Thank you. **Doctor:** Let&#x27;s refresh my memory. You had a stem cell transplant about a year before this all started, right? For myelodysplastic syndrome? **Patient:** That&#x27;s right. High risk myelodysplastic syndrome. **Doctor:** And after the transplant, you had some complications, like some nerve pain that went away on its own, and then some graft-versus-host disease affecting your skin and gut? **Patient:** Yes, it was pretty rough for a while there. But the GvHD cleared up, thankfully. **Doctor:** And then, around three months after the transplant, you developed a blood problem, something like hemolytic uremic syndrome? We treated that with plasmapheresis and eculizumab, correct? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s all correct. And around the same time, I had some weakness in my arms and legs. The doctors thought it was just from being so sick. **Doctor:** Right, critical illness polyneuropathy. But then, almost a year after the transplant, you started getting progressively weaker, especially in your legs, and you lost the ability to walk. You also noticed increasing numbness in all your limbs? **Patient:** Exactly! That&#x27;s when things got really bad. **Doctor:** So, we did a detailed neurological exam and nerve conduction studies, which showed a significant worsening of a severe axonal polyneuropathy compared to the previous tests. **Patient:** Yes, I remember those tests. They said my nerves were badly damaged. **Doctor:** We also noticed you were experiencing dry eyes and mouth, and your blood tests showed positive SSA(Ro) autoantibodies. So, we did a biopsy of your salivary glands. **Patient:** And that&#x27;s when you found the Sjogren&#x27;s, right? **Doctor:** Yes. The biopsy showed inflammation in your salivary glands, but not the kind of scarring we see with chronic GvHD. Based on all of that, we diagnosed you with chronic inflammatory polyneuropathy associated with Sjgren&#x27;s syndrome. **Patient:** So, that&#x27;s what was causing the weakness and numbness? **Doctor:** Precisely. That&#x27;s why we started you on intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) treatment. You&#x27;ve been getting it monthly, except for that one time you had a severe infection, right? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. And the IVIg has helped a lot. I feel much stronger. **Doctor:** Good! And the nerve conduction studies have shown improvement as well. At your last follow-up, which was about 29 months after starting the IVIg, the polyneuropathy was stable. So, we will continue with the IVIg treatment. Any questions?

MC7. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 63-year-old man visited our clinic for treatment of gastric HGD. The patient was referred to our clinic after a biopsy revealed a 1-cm active ulcerative lesion, identified as a microfocal tubular adenoma with HGD, after 2 months of proton pump inhibitor use and 1 week of Helicobacter pylori eradication treatment (Fig. 1). Upon physical examination, the patient exhibited no specific symptoms or abnormalities. The patient had no history of alcohol use or smoking, and no history of specifically diagnosed medical conditions. Blood tests showed that the white blood cell count was 5,340103/L; hemoglobin, 13.9 g/dL; and platelet count, 245103/L. All other laboratory findings were within normal limits. No specific gastric wall abnormalities or lymph node enlargements were noted on an abdominal computed tomography scan. The patient underwent a repeated upper endoscopy in our clinic for a more exact evaluation of the previously proven gastric dysplasia. A 7-mm, discolored, nodular lesion (previously found to be HGD) was noted at the lesser curvature side of the low body (Fig. 2A); therefore, we obtained two biopsy samples. A second elevated and nodular lesion was noted at the entire angle, and two biopsy samples were obtained from the more prominent nodular lesion located at the anterior wall of the angle (Fig. 2B). Both lesions revealed HGD; therefore, we planned to perform ESD for HGD treatment.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. Smith. Thanks for coming in. I understand you were referred to us for further evaluation of a biopsy result. **Mr. Smith:** Good morning, Doctor. Yes, that&#x27;s right. My previous doctor said something about dysplasia in my stomach. **Doctor:** Exactly. The biopsy showed a 1-centimeter ulcerative lesion that was diagnosed as a microfocal tubular adenoma with high-grade dysplasia, or HGD. This was after you had been on proton pump inhibitors for two months and a week of treatment to get rid of *Helicobacter pylori*. **Mr. Smith:** Okay, I remember all of that. **Doctor:** Now, when I examined you, you didn&#x27;t seem to be experiencing any specific symptoms or discomfort, is that correct? **Mr. Smith:** That&#x27;s right, Doctor. I feel fine. And I don&#x27;t drink or smoke, and I haven&#x27;t been diagnosed with any other medical conditions. **Doctor:** Good. Your blood tests came back normal as well. Your white blood cell count was 5,340, hemoglobin was 13.9, and platelets were 245. All other results were within the normal range. Also, the abdominal CT scan didn&#x27;t show any obvious problems with your stomach wall or any enlarged lymph nodes. **Mr. Smith:** So, what happens now? **Doctor:** Well, we performed another upper endoscopy here to get a better look at the area. We found a 7-millimeter discolored, nodular lesion on the lesser curvature side of the lower part of your stomach, which is where the previous HGD was found. We took two biopsies from that. **Mr. Smith:** And? **Doctor:** We also found another elevated, nodular lesion at the angle of your stomach. We took two biopsies from the most prominent part of that lesion. Unfortunately, both of these new biopsies also showed HGD. **Mr. Smith:** So, what does this all mean? **Doctor:** It means we need to treat this HGD. The best approach for you at this point is a procedure called Endoscopic Submucosal Dissection, or ESD. We&#x27;ll be removing those lesions to prevent them from potentially developing into something more serious.

MC8. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 35-year-old female patient was admitted with major joint pain and fatigue for 1 month. There were 74.55% blasts in the bone marrow (BM) detected by BM aspiration, and 70.71% abnormal B lymphocytes in the BM measured by flow cytometry (FCM). The patients leukemia cells expressed CD19+CD22+CD10+CD38+CD20 (Fig. 1A). She was diagnosed with B-cell ALL. Cytogenetic studies showed a complex karyotype: 47, XX, +5, t(12; 17) (p13, q21) [4]/47, idem, add (1) (p36.1), add (18) (q23), inc [cp6]/46, XX[15]. No genetic mutation was found in the leukemia cells. After two courses of induction therapy with vincristine, daunorubicin, cyclophosphamide, and prednisolone (VDCP), 1.98% leukemia blasts in the BM measured by FCM were revealed, which indicates there was no remission of B-cell acute lymphoblastic leukemia (B-ALL). Then a lumbar puncture and intrathecal injection of cytarabine, methotrexate, and dexamethasone were performed. After a following two courses of Hyper-CVAD B (methotrexate, leucovorin, and cytarabine) and one cycle of inter-median dose cytarabine chemotherapy, the patient never achieved complete remission (CR), and the leukemia cells in the BM reached 50.45% measured by FCM.<br><br>

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 60-year-old female, who was on CAPD for 7 years, presented with channel perforation of the Tenckhoff catheter. She had no mechanical or infective complication earlier. At presentation, she complained of leak from a perforation of Tenckhoff catheter. She was afebrile with stable vitals. On examination, there was a 2 mm longitudinal crack, 2 cm distal to the skin exit site of the catheter with stenosed distal channel. Peritoneal fluid leak was easily discernible at the site of the crack. There was no pericatheter leakage, edema, or induration at the exit site. Leukocyte count of catheter fluid was within normal limits and culture was sterile. Based on the clinical diagnosis of Tenckhoff catheter&#x27;s crack, we planned for partial reimplantation. The surgical procedure was performed under local anesthetic 1% lignocaine. We placed the skin incision over the previous scar of catheter insertion. Dissection was carried out along the proximal normal portion of catheter so as to gain adequate length from the looped internal part, which was then exteriorized along with outer Teflon cuff superior to the incision line. This partial replantation was accomplished by excision of catheter proximal to the crack site and fitting the tungsten adapter at the cut end [Figure 1]. Immediately following the procedure, CAPD was re-started and she is undergoing regular dialysis for last 6 months without any complications.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Smith. So, you&#x27;re here because you&#x27;ve noticed a problem with your dialysis catheter? **Patient:** Yes, Doctor. I&#x27;ve been on CAPD for about seven years now, and I&#x27;ve never had any issues before. But lately, I&#x27;ve noticed some fluid leaking from the catheter near where it comes out of my skin. **Doctor:** Okay. Can you tell me more about the leak? **Patient:** Well, it seems to be coming from a small hole or crack in the catheter itself. I haven&#x27;t had a fever, and I feel generally okay, just worried about the leak. **Doctor:** Alright, let&#x27;s take a look. *[Doctor examines the catheter]* I see what you mean. There&#x27;s a small crack, about 2 millimeters long, about 2 centimeters away from where the catheter exits your skin. It looks like the channel beyond the crack is a bit narrowed. And yes, I can see the peritoneal fluid leaking from there. The good news is, there&#x27;s no leakage or swelling around the exit site itself. We also checked the fluid and there&#x27;s no sign of infection. **Patient:** So, what does this mean? **Doctor:** It means the Tenckhoff catheter itself has a crack in it. Since it&#x27;s leaking, we need to fix it. We&#x27;re going to do a partial reimplantation of the catheter. **Patient:** What does that involve? **Doctor:** Basically, we&#x27;ll make a small incision over the old scar where the catheter was originally inserted. We&#x27;ll carefully expose the healthy part of the catheter inside, pull some extra length out, and bring it out through a new opening above the current one. Then we&#x27;ll cut off the damaged part of the catheter, the part with the crack, and connect the remaining good part to a connector. We&#x27;ll do all of this under local anesthetic, so you won&#x27;t feel any pain. **Patient:** And then I can go back to dialysis as normal? **Doctor:** Yes, we&#x27;ll restart the CAPD immediately after the procedure. And I&#x27;m happy to say that you&#x27;ve been undergoing regular dialysis for the last six months since the procedure, and you haven&#x27;t had any complications. Everything seems to be working well.

A 19-year-old girl presented to the Emergency Department at 1 am after having a football kicked from a distance of 510 m into her abdomen 7 h previously whilst watching a game. She complained of worsening diffuse abdominal pain and vomiting. Her observations were stable; however, on examination, her abdomen was diffusely tender and there was evidence of generalized peritonism. She had a white cell count of 18.8  109/l, but the rest of her blood tests, including amylase, was normal. Her chest radiograph did not show any evidence of free air under the diaphragm. A computed tomographic (CT) scan of her abdomen and pelvis was arranged, which showed abnormal areas of low attenuation and multiple pockets of air in the right flank, surrounding the right kidney and in the right sub-hepatic space, consistent with a perforation of either the ascending colon or the duodenum (Fig. 1). A laparotomy was performed and a 0.5 cm perforation was seen in the antero-lateral border of the duodenum at the junction of the first and second parts of the duodenum following kocherization. The perforation was oversewn and repaired with an omental patch. She recovered well from the operation and was discharged 4 days later. Figure 1:CT of abdomen showing free air in right para-renal space (white arrow).<br><br>

A 67-year-old man with chief complaints of macrohematuria and an abnormally low hemoglobin level (4.8 g/dL; normal range, 13.7-16.8 g/dL) was referred to our hospital. His performance status was zero and none of any co-morbidities were identified. Whole-body computed tomography (CT) revealed left hydronephrosis, a bladder tumor on the right lateral wall, and right external iliac LN involvement (Figure 1A). CT revealed a bladder tumor with invasion of surrounding fibroadipose tissue on the right lateral wall (Figure 1B). Transurethral resection of the bladder tumor was performed after blood transfusion; histopathological diagnosis revealed high-grade UC of the bladder with muscle layer invasion. BCa was classified as clinical T3bN1M0 according to the staging system defined in the American Joint Committee on Cancer Staging Manual [8]. His estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) was 64.57 mL/min and his renal function was maintained at a normal eGFR level during the medication for BCa. He received two combined courses of gemcitabine and cisplatin (GC; 1,000 mg/m2 gemcitabine on days 1, 8, and 15, and 70 mg/m2 cisplatin on day 2) every 21 days. To monitor the treatment effect on BCa, the patient underwent whole-body CT and pelvic MRI after every two courses of systemic therapy.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. Smith. Thanks for coming in. So, I understand you&#x27;ve been experiencing some blood in your urine? **Mr. Smith:** Yes, doctor. The CT scan also showed that the tumor is invading the tissue around your bladder. We&#x27;ll be closely monitoring you for any side effects and adjusting the treatment as needed. We&#x27;ll also keep a close eye on your kidney function throughout the treatment.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 63-year-old man with lung squamous carcinoma and right pleural metastasis received nivolumab therapy due to failure of the first-line chemotherapy.4,5 Clinically, it was found that the primary lesion achieved rapid and partial remission, but the secondary fibrosis became more complicated after nivolumab treatment. After administering methylprednisolone, the radiological findings showed carcinoma regression.6 Three weeks after starting the nivolumab treatment, the patient was noticed having serious neutropenia (0.431012/L), and newly developed fever as well as purulent sputum. Although neutropenia was completely cured by using G-CSF, infection by MDR-Kp was reported by microbiological culture assay. The strain is resistant to -lactams, sulfonamides and quinolones. In addition, occurrence of a new lesion in the lower lobe of the left lung suspected to be carcinoma metastasis was revealed by chest CT. At that point, an antibiotic therapy was decided to be administered and adjusted according to the susceptibility of MDR-Kp. Following treatment with tigecycline and meropenem for &lt;2 weeks, the lesion in the lower lobe of the left lung was well controlled (Figures 1 and 2). The patients legally authorized representative provided written informed consent for the patients information and images to be included in this report. This case report was approved by the Ethics and Review Committee of the First Affiliated Hospital of Soochow University.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 74-year-old Caucasian male (Table 1) with Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia was admitted to our medical intensive care unit for progressive respiratory failure. He had been undergoing cyclophosphamide pulse therapy for relapse of a known AAV. Kidney replacement therapy was required due to AKI in CKD. Due to a Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, he received treatment with co-trimoxazole turning negative for Pneumocystis jirovecii [8] so that immunosuppressive treatment with cyclophosphamide pulse therapy was continued (360 mg/m2; i.e., 72% of standard dose). To obtain maximum therapeutic but minimum side effect of cyclophosphamide, we used IHD followed by PIKRT both with a polysulphone high-flux dialyser (F60S, surface area 1.3 m2, Fresenius Medical Care). IHD was started 7 h after end of cyclophosphamide infusion with a blood/dialysate flow of 300 mL/h for 255 min, followed by PIKRT with a blood/dialysate flow of 140 mL/h for 540 min. Dialyser clearance was calculated from concentrations before (Cin) and after (Cout) the dialysis membrane as CLdial=FlinCinFloutCoutCin with plasma flow in (Flin) and out (Flout) of the dialyser estimated by blood flow, haematocrit, and ultrafiltration rate. All samples were centrifuged at 1,300 g for 10 min at 4C. Plasma was separated and stored at 80C until analysis. After fluid extraction (sodium carbonate/diethylether) and derivatization with trifluoracetic acid anhydride, the amount of cyclophosphamide was quantified by gas chromatography (Agilent GC 7890A) with mass spectrometric detection (Agilent MS 5975C) in the selected ion-monitoring mode. Measuring range of cyclophosphamide was from 0.005 (limit of detection) up to 200 mg/L with an intra-assay imprecision of 4.5%. For calibration, Ifosphamide was used as an internal standard.<br><br>

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 100-year-old female developed sudden onset severe right-sided paralysis and aphasia in the presence of her daughter at 15:00. She had persistent atrial fibrillation and was diagnosed with colon cancer a month earlier. She had no diabetes or dyslipidemia. She lived alone, and prior to the stroke, she was able to independently perform toileting and bathing and was generally independent in daily activities, with a modified Rankin Scale (mRS) score of 1. She was admitted to the hospital at 15:31 with a National Institutes of Health Stroke Scale (NIHSS) score of 24. Three-dimensional computed tomography (CT) angiography at 15:50 revealed occlusion of the M1 segment of the left middle cerebral artery with an Alberta Stroke Program Early CT Score of 10 (Figure 1A). She was treated with direct endovascular therapy without recombinant tissue plasminogen activator because of a recent history of bloody stool. Groin puncture was performed at 16:32, and a FlowGate Balloon Guide Catheter (8 Fr  85 cm; Stryker Neurovascular, Fremont, CA, USA) was placed in the left internal carotid artery (ICA). A Trevo 4  20 mm stent retriever (Stryker Neurovascular) was deployed from M2 to M1 while controlling blood flow on the proximal side. A Catalyst 6 aspiration catheter (Stryker Neurovascular) was placed in contact with the Trevo and removed together while applying aspiration pressure. A red thrombus was removed, and complete recanalization with thrombolysis in cerebral infarction grade 3 was achieved with the first-pass effect at 17:09, 129 min after symptom onset (Figures 1B, 1C). Neurological deficits improved immediately after treatment, with NIHSS scores of 8 and 4 on postintervention days 1 and 2, respectively. Magnetic resonance diffusion-weighted imaging performed two days later showed only a very small infarct (Figure 1D). Although slight motor aphasia remained, she was discharged home after eight days of hospitalization, with an mRS of 2 (Videos 1, 2).<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, I&#x27;m Dr. Smith. I understand you&#x27;re the daughter of our patient? **Daughter:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. <strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 40-year-old female with negative smoking and medical history was admitted with productive cough, progressively aggravating dyspnea on exertion, and recurrent pulmonary infections for the previous 2 months. The chest X-ray showed total atelectasis of the left lung (Figure 1), and the thoracic CT-scan revealed a mass of the left hemithorax, which probably originated in the mediastinum and extended to the whole left pleural space (Figure 2). The mass showed heterogeneous density containing soft tissue elements, fat, cystic areas and foci of calcification, which is the classic imaging appearance of a benign teratoma on CT. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) was performed to specify the anatomic relationships and confirm the tumor morphologic features. The MRI yielded useful information about the vital structures of the mediastinum, whether invaded or externally compressed by the tumor. Specifically, the MRI confirmed a round, non-homogeneous, well circumscribed mass of a 12 cm diameter, exerting compression on the mediastinum great vessels and the left hilar structures (vessels and airway) (Figure 3). The bronchoscopy found stenosis of the trachea by external compression, and narrowing in the foramen of the left main bronchus resulting in difficulty in the insertion of the bronchoscope. The mediastinal tumor markers (-fetoprotein and -human chorionic gonadotropin) were both normal. As the findings of CT and MRI suggested a benign teratoma, a complete resection was contemplated. Since the mass was supposed to be respectable, surgical management was first in the priority list of therapeutic options. The cytological examination through transcutaneous needle aspiration or biopsy of the tumor were considered redundant and were omitted, because of the dispersion risk and the necessity for total removal so as to ameliorate the respiratory function and re-expand the left lung. Moreover, needle biopsy allows examination of only a small amount of tissue and may be inadequate for definitive diagnosis [5]. As already underlined in the literature, diagnosis and therapy rely on surgical excision, and even with large sized tumors whose complete resection is impossible, partial resection still relieves symptoms, frequently without relapse [6]. Pulmonary function tests were impaired: FVC 1.19 (36.7% of predicted) and FEV1 1.01 (41.5% of predicted), whereas a-FP, CG were normal.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning. So, you&#x27;re here today because you&#x27;ve been having some trouble breathing and a persistent cough, is that right? **Patient:** Yes, doctor. For the past two months, I&#x27;ve had a cough that&#x27;s producing a lot of phlegm, and I&#x27;m getting really short of breath, especially when I try to do anything. I&#x27;ve also had a few lung infections in that time. **Doctor:** Okay. And you&#x27;re 40 years old, and you don&#x27;t smoke, and you have no other medical history we need to be aware of? **Patient:** That&#x27;s correct. **Doctor:** Alright. We did some imaging tests. Your chest X-ray showed that your left lung has collapsed. And the CT scan revealed a mass in the left side of your chest, likely originating in the mediastinum. It seems to be extending into the whole left pleural space. **Patient:** A mass? What kind of mass? **Doctor:** The CT scan shows it has different densities, with soft tissue, fat, cystic areas, and some calcification. This looks like a classic benign teratoma on the CT. We also did an MRI to get a better look at the anatomy and confirm the tumor&#x27;s features. It&#x27;s round, non-homogeneous, well-defined, and about 12 cm in diameter. It&#x27;s compressing the major blood vessels in your chest and the structures near your left lung, including the vessels and airway. **Patient:** So, what does that mean for my breathing? **Doctor:** We also performed a bronchoscopy, which is a scope down your airway. It showed that the mass is pressing on your trachea from the outside, causing it to narrow. It&#x27;s also narrowing the opening to your left main bronchus, which made it difficult to even insert the scope. Your pulmonary function tests confirm this, showing your lung capacity is significantly reduced. Your FVC is 1.19, which is only about 37% of what we&#x27;d expect, and your FEV1 is 1.01, around 42% of the predicted value. **Patient:** Okay. So, it&#x27;s definitely a tumor? Is it cancerous? **Doctor:** The good news is that the mediastinal tumor markers, -fetoprotein and -human chorionic gonadotropin, came back normal. Combined with the CT and MRI findings, it strongly suggests this is a benign teratoma, not cancerous. **Patient:** So, what&#x27;s the plan? **Doctor:** Because it appears to be a benign teratoma and is causing significant breathing problems, we are recommending complete surgical removal of the mass. We feel that a biopsy is unnecessary at this point. Removing the entire mass should improve your breathing and allow your left lung to re-expand. Even if we can&#x27;t remove all of it, removing a portion will relieve the symptoms. **Patient:** Surgery sounds scary. Is there any other option? **Doctor:** In this case, surgery is the best approach. It&#x27;s the most direct way to alleviate the compression on your airway and vessels and allow your lung to function properly again. We believe the mass is removable, and we&#x27;ll do everything we can to ensure a successful outcome.

A 33-year-old patient was referred to our skull-base center because of a large vestibular schwannoma (VS) (diameter of 4.4 cm, Figure 1: magnetic resonance imaging). The patient presented with unilateral hearing loss, mild ataxia, and signs of cerebellar coordination dysfunction after discussing the case in our multidisciplinary team, we advised a planned partial resection via the translabyrinthine approach. The patient gave informed consent and was admitted for surgery, which was performed by an ENT/Neurosurgery team. The facial nerve was EMG-monitored during surgery. The surgery was without complications and a substantial amount of tumor was removed. The patient showed no additional deficits soon after the surgery and displayed good recovery in the next 24 h. However, in the evening of the 2nd day, the patient developed a cardiac arrest which was followed by a Glasgow Coma Score of 1-1-1 in a short period of time. The patient was swiftly resuscitated and a computed tomography (CT) scan was made. CT showed generalized brain edema (supratentorial and infratentorial, Figure 2) and the presence of blood in the resection cavity. Venous sinus thrombosis was ruled out as a cause of delayed cerebellar hemorrhage and swelling. The blood in the resection cavity was decompressed immediately and the patient was transferred to the intensive care unit, after which another CT was made. The second CT showed evacuation of the hematoma but progression of the generalized brain edema. Subsequently, the patient died and an autopsy was performed. The autopsy indicated generalized lung edema, brain edema, and Hashimotos thyroiditis (HT), and a diagnosis of neurogenic lung edema was made. Histological examination of the tumor confirmed the diagnosis of schwannoma.<br><br>

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 32-year-old woman presented to our outpatient psychiatry clinic with episodes of insomnia, disorganized speech, and persecutory delusions, as described by her mother. The patient had inadequate self-care and blunt affection during her mental assessment. She was normally oriented to space, time, and people. She was found to experience commanding auditory hallucinations. During examination high-amplitude, arrhythmic, postural, and kinetic tremors were observed in both hands. At the same time, the patient had repetitive chewing movements. She smacked her lips occasionally, and there was a behavior of opening and closing her mouth. These movements were involuntary, uncontrollable, and arrhythmic. On the basis of the fifth edition of the Statistical and Diagnostic Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM-5), schizophrenia was diagnosed following a psychiatric evaluation. The positive and negative syndrome scale (PANSS) was used to evaluate clinical symptoms. The patients PANSS scores were as follows: positive scale 26, negative scale 28, and general psychopathology scale 44. Her initial clinical global impressionseverity of illness (CGISI) score was 6. She was taking only valproate 1000 mg/day when she presented to us; this drug was prescribed for her epilepsy at age 10. Since status epilepticus was observed in the patients first epileptic seizure, antiepileptic medication was started immediately. The patient did not have any epileptic seizures afterward, but valproate treatment was never stopped because the patients mother also had epilepsy and due to the patients history of status epilepticus. She had subsequently been prescribed risperidone (up to 10 mg/day) at age 22, paliperidone injection (100 mg once monthly) and olanzapine (up to 20 mg/day) at age 27, and then aripiprazole (up to 30 mg/day) at age 30, which had been maintained at therapeutic levels for over 8 weeks. Nonetheless, her complaints did not get any better. In addition, tardive dyskinetic movements appeared at the age of 28 when taking paliperidone injections and oral olanzapine.<br><br>

And how have you been feeling about taking care of yourself lately? **Patient:** (Shrugs) **Doctor:** I see. And can you tell me if you know where you are right now, what the day is, and who I am? **Patient:** (Quietly) Yes. Im at the doctor. I know what day it is. I know who you are. **Doctor:** That&#x27;s good. Have you been hearing any voices? **Patient:** (Hesitates) Sometimes. They tell me what to do. **Doctor:** Okay. I&#x27;m also noticing some movements in your hands, a tremor. And you seem to be chewing and smacking your lips a bit. Are you aware of those movements? **Patient:** (Frowning) I can&#x27;t really control them. **Doctor:** I see. Based on everything you&#x27;ve told me and what I&#x27;ve observed, and considering the symptoms like the disorganized speech, hallucinations, and these movements, I believe you&#x27;re experiencing schizophrenia. To get a better sense of how severe things are, I&#x27;ve used a scale called the PANSS. Your scores are 26 on the positive scale, 28 on the negative scale, and 44 on the general psychopathology scale. Also, your CGI-SI score, which measures the overall severity of your illness, is 6. **Doctor:** Now, I see from your records that you&#x27;re currently taking valproate, 1000 mg a day. It says here it was prescribed for epilepsy when you were 10. **Patient:** Yeah, I had a really bad seizure back then. **Doctor:** I understand. It says you haven&#x27;t had any seizures since, but the valproate was continued because of your history of status epilepticus and because your mother also has epilepsy. And you&#x27;ve also been prescribed other medications, like risperidone, paliperidone injections, olanzapine, and aripiprazole, but they havent really helped. **Patient:** No, they haven&#x27;t. **Doctor:** I see here that you developed some of these involuntary movements, what we call tardive dyskinesia, around age 28, when you were taking paliperidone and olanzapine.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 61-year-old male was referred to our burn center with burn injuries. The burn percentage was 20% Total Burn Surface Area (TBSA) and IIb-III degree, involving thorax, abdomen, and right arm. Patient was treated with split-thickness skin graft. We discharged the patient after successful management with healed graft and nearly healed donor wounds. 8 months later, the patient was referred to us from his primary care physician for management of infected open wounds in donor sites. Physical examination revealed infected area with hypergranulated tissue in donor sites, left upper limb and left lower limb (Figure 1 (Fig. 1)). Wound cultures showed Staphylococcus aureus, Escherichia coli and Staphylococcus epidermidis (Methicillin-resistent, MRSE) bacteria. Patient was a heavy smoker and suffered from multiple sclerosis. Up to our knowledge, neglect and inadequate wound care were responsible for the opening and secondary infection of donor sites. One day after admission we performed a surgical debridement and removed the hypertrophic granulation tissue. After the operation we started daily wound dressing with a non-adherent antimicrobial alginate dressing (Silvercell) and irradiation with water-filtered infrared-A (wIRA). Water-filtered infrared-A (wIRA) protocols used at our department were radiations all wound areas three times daily with a 60 cm distance for 30 minutes with a Hydrosun 505 model (Hydrosun Medizintechnik, Mllheim, Germany). The patient tolerated this application well without any complaints. After 5 weeks the patient was discharged with healed donor sites left thigh and left lower leg and nearly totally healed sites left forearm and left upper arm (Figure 2 (Fig. 2)).<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. Smith. I see you&#x27;re here about some issues with the donor sites from your skin graft surgery last year. **Mr. Smith:** Yes, Doctor. My family doctor sent me over. About eight months ago you had a 20% TBSA burn, with IIb-III degree burns on your chest, abdomen, and right arm. We treated that with a split-thickness skin graft, and you were discharged with healed grafts and nearly healed donor sites. **Mr. Smith:** That&#x27;s right. Everything seemed okay for a while. **Doctor:** I understand you&#x27;re having problems with those donor sites now. Can you tell me more? **Mr. Smith:** They just haven&#x27;t closed up properly and seem to be infected. **Doctor:** Okay. Let&#x27;s take a look. ( *Doctor examines the patient* ) I see open wounds on your left arm and leg, with some excessive tissue growth. We&#x27;ll need to take a wound culture. **(Later, after culture results are in)** **Doctor:** Okay, Mr. Smith, the wound cultures came back. I understand you also have multiple sclerosis and are a heavy smoker? **Mr. Smith:** Yes, that&#x27;s correct. **Doctor:** Based on what I&#x27;m seeing and the history, it seems like inadequate wound care after your discharge led to these sites opening up and becoming infected. We&#x27;re going to admit you to the hospital. **Mr. Smith:** Alright, what&#x27;s the plan? **Doctor:** Tomorrow, we&#x27;ll perform a surgical debridement to clean the wounds and remove that excess tissue. After that, we&#x27;ll be doing daily wound dressings with a special non-adherent antimicrobial alginate dressing called Silvercell. We&#x27;ll also be using water-filtered infrared-A, or wIRA, to help with healing. **Mr. Smith:** Water-filtered infrared-A? What&#x27;s that involve? **Doctor:** We&#x27;ll be exposing the wound areas to the wIRA three times a day for 30 minutes each time, from about 60 cm away, using a Hydrosun 505 device. It&#x27;s a gentle treatment and most patients tolerate it very well. **Mr. Smith:** Okay, sounds good. **(Five weeks later)** **Doctor:** Mr. Smith, I&#x27;m happy to say you&#x27;ve made excellent progress. The donor sites on your left thigh and lower leg are completely healed, and the ones on your left forearm and upper arm are almost completely healed. **Mr. Smith:** That&#x27;s great news, Doctor! **Doctor:** Yes, you&#x27;ll be discharged today. It&#x27;s crucial that you follow our instructions for wound care very carefully this time. We&#x27;ll go over them again before you leave. And please, consider quitting smoking. It significantly impacts wound healing and your overall health.

A 57-year-old male with a history of hypertension and smoking habit suffered from cerebral infarction of the right cerebrum due to ICA occlusion. MRA after 8 months showed mild stenosis of the right ICA in the neck [Figure 1d]. MRA after 9 months also showed no cerebral aneurysm on the left ICA [Figure 1e]. MRA obtained 38 months after cerebral infarction showed a small protrusion on the left ICA at the origin of the PComA. The size of the protrusion was about 2 mm. The left PComA was thick, as observed on the initial radiological examinations [Figure 1f]. Gas chromatography/mass spectrometry (GS/MS) analysis showed extremely high concentrations of methylmalonic acid in urine. In subsequent evaluations, the patient showed significant improvement in her overall condition.<br><br>

MC66. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 21-year-old female, who has underwent renal transplantation 1 week ago, was referred to the department of nuclear medicine for renal transplant scintigraphy. She had end stage renal disease as a consequence of nephritic syndrome for the past 4 years, and was maintained on regular hemodialysis. Both her previous right and left brachial-cephalic arterio-venous fistulas had become dysfunctional, and she had been on a renal transplant waiting list since then. A catheter had been implanted in her right internal jugular vein (IJV), and she underwent hemodialysis via this route for 10 months. The catheter has been removed a few days after transplantation. Dynamic renal scintigraphy was obtained in an anterior view after bolus injection of 7 mCi 99mTechnetium-diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid (99mTc-DTPA) through an 18-gauge venous cannula in the right antecubital vein intravenously. The images were obtained with a single-head gamma camera (Siemens ecam) fitted with a low energy high-resolution collimator. In the angiographic phase, sequential 1-second images revealed an unusual vascular filling pattern along with patent iliac arteries and transplanted kidney (Figure 1). In order to gather further information on the abnormal finding, an additional angioscintigraphy of the chest in the anterior view was performed following bolus injection of 5 mCi 99mTc-DTPA through the same venous cannula 4 hours later (Figure 2). Sequential 1-second images demonstrated rapid filling of the right basilic and cephalic veins followed by the axillary and subclavian veins (Figure 2). Unexpectedly, two parallel channels appeared and the right heart, left heart and aorta became visible in turn. Regurgitation into the lateral thoracic and IJVs was also noted (Figure 2). All findings were suggestive of SVC obstruction, which was attributed to the prolonged IJV catheterization, accompanied by blood flow through the azygos system (Figure 2). The patient did not have any symptoms or signs, except some dilated collateral veins over the chest and abdominal wall that were retrospectively detected on physical examination. Due to the risk of contrast nephropathy and the patients being asymptomatic, the referring physician did not agree to perform computed tomography (CT) angiography to obtain detailed anatomical information.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, good morning. I see you&#x27;re a 21-year-old female. You&#x27;re here for a renal transplant scintigraphy. I understand you had a kidney transplant about a week ago? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. **Doctor:** Okay. And you&#x27;ve had end-stage renal disease for the past four years because of nephritic syndrome, and you&#x27;ve been on hemodialysis? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s correct. My fistulas stopped working, and I was on the transplant list for a while. **Doctor:** I see here you had a catheter placed in your right internal jugular vein for hemodialysis for about 10 months, and it was removed a few days after your transplant. We performed a dynamic renal scintigraphy using a radioactive tracer, 99mTc-DTPA, injected into a vein in your right arm. **Patient:** That&#x27;s what I remember. **Doctor:** The initial images showed an unusual blood flow pattern around the kidney, but the iliac arteries and the kidney itself were working. So, we did another scan of your chest, also using 99mTc-DTPA. **Patient:** And what did that show? **Doctor:** That scan showed rapid filling of the veins in your right arm, then the axillary and subclavian veins. We also saw two parallel channels appear, then the right and left sides of your heart and the aorta. We also noticed some backflow into the veins in your chest and internal jugular veins. **Patient:** Is that bad? **Doctor:** It suggests you have a Superior Vena Cava (SVC) obstruction. It&#x27;s likely caused by the prolonged catheterization in your internal jugular vein. The blood is flowing through another system, called the azygos system, to get back to the heart. **Patient:** So, what does that mean for me? I haven&#x27;t really felt anything besides some veins being more visible. **Doctor:** Exactly. On examination, we did notice some dilated veins on your chest and abdomen, which we likely missed before. But you don&#x27;t have any other symptoms. Ideally, we would do a CT angiography to get a better picture of the obstruction. However, your referring physician doesn&#x27;t want to do that at this time because of the risk of damaging your new kidney, especially since you&#x27;re not experiencing any symptoms.

Pertinent concomitant medications included oral gabapentin 900 mg twice daily for anxiety, mood, and alcohol cravings (with poor adherence) and nicotine gum for cravings. A 40-year-old male from Bihar presented with multiple nodules around joints of extremities of 2 years duration and erythematous skin infiltration of face, trunk, and upper extremities of 1 year duration. <strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 61-year-old. man was admitted in our stroke unit after 2 reversible episodes of left monocular blindness associated with a total right distal upper limb paralysis. His medical past history was hypertension, hypercholesterolemia, and tobacco use. At the admission the clinical examination showed a total right wrist extension paresis, the so-called pseudoradial paresis related to a small left frontal infarction visible on MRI (DWI). The MR angiography (MRA) and the duplex scanning showed an occlusion of the left internal carotid artery due to atherosclerosis and a negative flow in the ophthalmic artery downstream. The intracranial MRA showed poor collateral supply by anterior communicating artery and an apparent nonfunctional posterior communicating artery. We decided to apply LBPP process for 2 reasons: (1) the worsening of the right hand deficit (see movie), (2) this worsening was not related to a fall in systemic blood pressure (167/84 mmHg). The LBPP was applied once during 90 minutes following the same procedure than in case one. The repeated measurements of blood pressure and heart rate did not show any significant changes along the procedure. The transcranial Doppler showed a decreased of the left MCA resistance to flow index ([systolic velocity-diastolic velocity]/systolic velocity, 0.5 at baseline to 0.4 at the end of the procedure) while there was slight changes on the MCA velocities (64/32 to 75/45 cm/sec). The left ophthalmic artery flow remained negative during and after LBPP application. Heart rate and blood pressure remained stable during LBPP application. The clinical improvement was sustained after anti-G suit deflation (as presented on the movie) consisting in the recovery of the pseudoradial palsy with persisting ataxia lasting few days. The total recovery was obtained before discharge.<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. [Patient&#x27;s Last Name]. Thanks for coming in. I understand you&#x27;ve been experiencing some issues. Can you tell me about them? **Patient:** Good morning, Doctor. Yes, I&#x27;ve had a couple of episodes where I suddenly lost vision in my left eye. And each time, my right arm, specifically my wrist, went completely weak. It&#x27;s been very worrying. **Doctor:** I understand. And you mentioned these episodes were reversible, meaning the vision and arm strength came back? **Patient:** Yes, they did, but it&#x27;s still concerning. **Doctor:** Absolutely. Now, I see in your history that you have hypertension, high cholesterol, and you&#x27;re a smoker. Is that correct? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. **Doctor:** Okay. So, when you came in, we did a physical exam and noticed weakness in your right wrist extension, what we call &quot;pseudoradial paresis.&quot; An MRI showed a small stroke, or infarction, in the left frontal area of your brain, which explains the symptoms. **Patient:** A stroke? I was afraid of that. **Doctor:** Yes, a small one. We also did an MR angiogram and a duplex scan, which revealed a blockage in your left internal carotid artery due to atherosclerosis, hardening of the arteries. Unfortunately, the blood flow in the ophthalmic artery, which supplies your eye, was negative downstream of the blockage. The intracranial MRA also showed poor collateral blood flow from the anterior communicating artery and a non-functional posterior communicating artery. **Patient:** So, what does all that mean, Doctor? **Doctor:** It means that the blockage in your carotid artery is affecting blood flow to your brain and eye, causing your symptoms. Because the weakness in your hand was worsening, and it wasn&#x27;t due to low blood pressure  your blood pressure was actually quite high at 167/84  we decided to try something called Lower Body Positive Pressure, or LBPP. **Patient:** LBPP? What&#x27;s that? **Doctor:** It&#x27;s a treatment where we applied pressure to your lower body, using a special suit, to help increase blood flow to your brain. We applied it for 90 minutes. During the procedure, your blood pressure and heart rate remained stable. We used transcranial Doppler to monitor the blood flow in your brain, and we saw a slight improvement in the resistance of blood flow in the left MCA. However, the ophthalmic artery flow remained negative. **Patient:** And did it work? **Doctor:** Yes, it did! After we removed the suit, you showed significant improvement in the right wrist weakness. You did have some ataxia, or unsteadiness, for a few days, but you made a full recovery before you were discharged. **Patient:** That&#x27;s wonderful news, Doctor! So, what happens now? Do I need more LBPP? **Doctor:** For now, the focus will be on managing your hypertension and high cholesterol, and of course, quitting smoking. We&#x27;ll discuss medications and lifestyle changes to prevent future strokes. We will also need to monitor the blockage in your carotid artery and consider further interventions if necessary.

A 32-year-old male with a history of a cyanotic CHD and ulcerative colitis presented for a laparoscopic total colectomy. <strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 58-year-old diabetic woman was admitted to emergency department with unstable angina. After initial medication with acetylsalicylic acid, clopidogrel, enoxaparin, atorvastatin, ramipril, and pantoprazole, she underwent coronary angiography which revealed 90% stenosis at circumflex coronary artery and obtuse marginal branch of circumflex coronary artery (CX-OM) bifurcation (Medina 1-1-1) (Figure 1). A 7 F Extra BackUp guiding catheter was used to engage the left main coronary artery. Both the main branch and the side branch were wired. The obtuse marginal branch (OM) was dilated with a 2.0  20-mm balloon dilatation catheter (Simpass; Simeks, Istanbul, Turkey) at 16 atm, and the circumflex coronary artery (Cx) body was dilated with a 2.5  20-mm balloon dilatation catheter (Simpass; Simeks) at 18 atm. Thereafter, while parking a balloon in the main branch, a 2.5  18-mm stent (Endeavor Resolute; Medtronic) was deployed in the side branch at 12 atm and postdilated with a 2.5  12-mm noncompliant (NC) balloon (Simpass; Simeks) at 20 atm. Subsequently deploying the main branch balloon, a mini crush was performed and another 2.5  30-mm stent (Endeavor Resolute; Medtronic) was implanted at 20 atm into the Cx body. The OM was rewired and final kissing was performed with NC balloons. Thereafter, localized haziness suggesting thrombosis at first sight appeared in the main-branch stent, both in the proximal part and distal to the OM take off (Figure 2). The patient had no symptom and was hemodynamically stable. StentBoost showed stent fracture and disruption (Figure 3). Another Endeavor Resolute stent was implanted at 14 atm to cover the fracture sites. The final result was satisfactory (Figure 4).<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Smith. I understand you came to the emergency department with chest pain. Can you tell me more about that? **Patient:** Yes, doctor. I&#x27;ve been having quite severe chest pain. **Doctor:** I see. And you mentioned you&#x27;re 58 and have diabetes, is that correct? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. **Doctor:** Okay. So, when you came in, we started you on some medications: acetylsalicylic acid, clopidogrel, enoxaparin, atorvastatin, ramipril, and pantoprazole, to stabilize things. We also performed a coronary angiography, which is an imaging procedure to look at your heart arteries. **Patient:** What did that show? **Doctor:** It revealed a significant blockage. You had a 90% narrowing at the point where the circumflex coronary artery and its branch, the obtuse marginal branch, meet. We call it Medina 1-1-1. **Patient:** So, what did you do about it? **Doctor:** We used a guiding catheter to access the left main coronary artery. Then, we carefully placed wires into both the main branch and the side branch that were blocked. We then used small balloons to open up the blockage in the obtuse marginal branch and the circumflex coronary artery. **Patient:** And then? **Doctor:** After that, we placed a stent  a small mesh tube  in the side branch, the obtuse marginal branch, to keep it open. We then placed another stent in the main branch, the circumflex coronary artery, using a technique called &quot;mini crush&quot; to ensure both branches were properly supported. **Patient:** Was that the end of it? **Doctor:** Not quite. After placing the stents, we saw some haziness on the images, which suggested a possible blood clot within the main branch stent. You weren&#x27;t having any symptoms and your blood pressure was stable, but further imaging showed a fracture and disruption of the stent. **Patient:** Oh dear, what did you do about that? **Doctor:** We implanted another stent to cover the fracture site. Fortunately, the final result looked satisfactory. **Patient:** So, everything is okay now? **Doctor:** Yes, the procedure went well in the end. We&#x27;ll keep you monitored and discuss your ongoing management plan.

A 53-year-old man was hospitalized for paralysis in the lower extremities. Computed tomography (CT) and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) findings revealed multiple bilateral lung nodules, swollen mediastinal lymph nodes, and osteolysis of thoracic vertebrae (Figure 1). A spinal decompression was performed, and vertebral biopsies were taken. Before 10 years, a round mediastinal tumor, 3.5 cm in diameter and proximate to the 11th thoracic vertebra, had been removed from the patient and was diagnosed as a benign paraganglioma of the mediastinum. Comparison of the vertebral biopsy specimen with tumor tissue from the initial surgery revealed that both specimens were pathologically identical, and the vertebral tumor was diagnosed as a metastasis from the mediastinal tumor. Histologically, the tumors showed a characteristic pseudoalveolar pattern, composed of large polygonal tumor cells with abundant cytoplasm, vesicular nuclei, marked nucleoli, and low nuclear cytoplasmic ratio (Figure 2(a)). The tumor tissue was re-evaluated using immunohistochemical (IHC) staining. The results were positive for TFE3 (Figure 2(b)) and S100 protein and very focally positive for CD10. However, staining was negative for the neuroendocrine markers, chromogranin A, dopamine beta-hydroxylase, synaptophysin, and CD56. The final diagnosis was revised to alveolar soft-part sarcoma (ASPS) of the mediastinum, with multiple metastases in the lungs, lymph nodes, and vertebrae. The patient died 4 months following biopsy.<br><br>

He was shifted to the pediatric intensive care unit and started on noninvasive ventilation. Diuretics were started and the distress gradually improved. Once stable, he was started on after-load reduction with enalapril. He gradually became better and was eventually discharged. However, we will need to perform surgery to repair his mitral valve. We waited three months until he was strong enough and then performed the surgery. He is now doing well and we are keeping a close eye on him with regular follow-up appointments.
